Figure 4.
The Jacobi diagrams obtained from necklaces with arrow in Figure
3
.

Also, we define a map
\mathfrak{mh}\colon\overrightarrow{N}_{2m}^{\prime\prime}\to\langle B_{2m-1}^{s}\rangle
by merging the two adjacent beads close to the head of the arrow, namely

\mathfrak{mh}(O(a_{1},\dots,a_{m}\uparrow a_{m},\dots,a_{1}))=O(a_{1},\dots,a_{m},\dots,a_{1}).

Furthermore, this map induces an isomorphism
\mathfrak{mh}\colon(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})\overrightarrow{N}_{2m}^{\prime\prime}\to\langle B_{2m-1}^{s}\rangle
by
[
15
, Proposition 5.2]
.
Similarly,
\mathfrak{mht}\colon\overrightarrow{N}_{2m}^{\prime\prime}\to\langle B_{2m-2}^{s}\rangle
is defined by merging the two beads close to the head and merging two beads close to the tail.

For each
x\in\overrightarrow{N}_{2m}
, there is a unique non-negative integer
e=e(x)
such that
x=x^{\pi}=\dots=x^{\pi/2^{e-1}}\neq x^{\pi/2^{e}}
, where
x^{\pi/2^{k}}
denotes the necklace with arrow obtained by rotating only the arrow of
x
by
\pi/2^{k}
.

Definition 5.2
.

Let
\iota\colon\overrightarrow{N}_{2m}\to\overrightarrow{N}_{2m}
be the map defined by
\iota(x)=x^{\pi/2^{e}}
.

Example 5.3
.

For
a\neq b
, let
x=O(a,b,b,a,a,b,b,a\uparrow a,b,b,a,a,b,b,a)\in\overrightarrow{N}_{16}
.
Then
e(x)=2
,
\iota(x)=O(b,a,a,b,b,a,a,b\uparrow b,a,a,b,b,a,a,b)
, and

\displaystyle\mathfrak{mh}(x)

\displaystyle=O(a,b,b,a,a,b,b,a,b,b,a,a,b,b,a),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{mh}(\iota(x))

\displaystyle=O(b,a,a,b,b,a,a,b,a,a,b,b,a,a,b)\in\mathcal{A}_{15,1}^{c,s}.

Lemma 5.4
.

The map
\iota
is an involution without fixed point.

Proof.

The map
\iota
is fixed point free by definition.
Let
y=\iota(x)
and
e=e(x)
.
Then for
k=0,1,\dots,e-1
we have
y^{\pi/2^{k}}=(x^{\pi/2^{k}})^{\pi/2^{e}}=x^{\pi/2^{e}}=y
.
Also,
y^{\pi/2^{e}}=x^{\pi/2^{e-1}}=x
, and thus,
\iota(y)=x
.
∎

Since
\#\overrightarrow{N}_{2m}^{\prime}=(2g)^{m+1}
and
\#\overrightarrow{N}_{2m}^{\prime\prime}=(2g)^{m}
, Lemma
5.4
implies that
\#(\overrightarrow{N}_{2m}/\iota)=\frac{1}{2}((2g)^{m+1}+(2g)^{m})
.
It follows from the proof of
[
15
, Proposition 5.2]
that
\operatorname{rank}_{\mathbb{Z}}\langle B_{2m}^{s}\rangle=\frac{1}{2}(2g+1)(2g)^{m}
.
Therefore, a natural map
\mathbb{Z}\overrightarrow{N}_{2m}\twoheadrightarrow\langle B_{2m}^{s}\rangle
induces an isomorphism
\mathbb{Z}(\overrightarrow{N}_{2m}/\iota)\to\langle B_{2m}^{s}\rangle
.
This isomorphism enables us to identify connected symmetric Jacobi diagrams with necklaces with arrow.

We consider the composite map

\mathcal{A}_{2m-1,1}^{c}\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{s}}Y_{2m-1}\mathcal{I}\mathcal{C}/Y_{2m}\xrightarrow{\bar{z}_{2m,2}}\mathcal{A}_{2m,2}^{c}\otimes\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}

Since the image of this homomorphism is included in
\mathcal{A}_{2m,2}^{c}\otimes(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}/\mathbb{Z})
, one obtains a homomorphism

\mathcal{A}_{2m-1,1}^{c}\xrightarrow{(\bar{z}_{2m,2}\circ\mathfrak{s})\otimes 2}\mathcal{A}_{2m,2}^{c}\otimes\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}.

Moreover, it induces a homomorphism

f\colon\mathcal{A}_{2m-1,1}^{c}\to(\mathcal{A}_{2m,2}^{c}/\langle\Theta_{2m}^{\geq 1}\rangle)\otimes\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{bd}}\mathcal{A}_{2m-2,1}^{c}\otimes\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z},

which sends
O(a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{m},\dots,a_{2},a_{1})
to
O(a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{m},\dots,a_{2})
by
[
15
, Theorem 1.1]
.

Let us prove Theorem
1.1
:
\operatorname{rank}_{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}\operatorname{Ker}(\pi\circ\mathfrak{s}_{2m-1,1})=\frac{1}{2}((2g)^{m}-(2g)^{\lceil m/2\rceil})
for
m\geq 2
.
Note that
\operatorname{Ker}(\pi\circ\mathfrak{s}_{2m-1,1})\subset\operatorname{tor}\mathcal{A}_{2m-1,1}^{c}
.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

It follows from Corollary
3.17
that

\left\langle\mathfrak{mh}(x)+\mathfrak{mh}(\iota(x))\in\langle B_{2m-1}^{s}\rangle\Bigm|x\in\overrightarrow{N}_{2m}^{e=0}\right\rangle\subset\operatorname{Ker}(\pi\circ\mathfrak{s}_{2m-1,1}),

where
\overrightarrow{N}_{2m}^{e=0}=\{x\in\overrightarrow{N}^{\prime\prime}_{2m}\mid e(x)=0\}
.
We prove that this inclusion is actually an equality, and then the proof is complete since the rank of the
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-module on the left is
\frac{1}{2}((2g)^{m}-(2g)^{\lceil m/2\rceil})
.

Since
\bar{z}_{2m,1}
factors through
\pi
, we obtain
\operatorname{Ker}(\pi\circ\mathfrak{s}_{2m-1,1})\subset\operatorname{Ker}(\bar{z}_{2m,1}\circ\mathfrak{s}_{2m-1,1})
.
It follows from
[
15
, Theorem 1.1]
that for
J=O(a_{1},\dots,a_{m-1},a_{m},a_{m-1},\dots,a_{1})
we have

\displaystyle\bar{z}_{2m,1}\circ\mathfrak{s}(J)=\delta^{\prime}(J)=\frac{1}{2}O(a_{1},\dots,a_{m-1},a_{m},a_{m},a_{m-1},\dots,a_{1}).

Therefore, one obtains a commutative diagram

\textstyle{(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})\overrightarrow{N}_{2m}^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{mh}}
\textstyle{(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})(\overrightarrow{N}_{2m}/\iota)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\langle B_{2m-1}^{s}\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{z}_{2m,1}\circ\mathfrak{s}}
\textstyle{\langle B_{2m}^{s}\rangle\otimes\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z},}

where the vertical arrows are the isomorphisms explained above.
We now conclude that

\operatorname{Ker}(\bar{z}_{2m,1}\circ\mathfrak{s}_{2m-1,1})\subset\left\langle\mathfrak{mh}(x)+\mathfrak{mh}(\iota(x))\Bigm|x,\iota(x)\in\overrightarrow{N}^{\prime\prime}_{2m}\right\rangle.

When
m
is odd, this completes the proof since
\overrightarrow{N}_{2m}^{e=0}
coincides with
\{x\in\overrightarrow{N}^{\prime\prime}_{2m}\mid\iota(x)\in\overrightarrow{N}^{\prime\prime}_{2m}\}
.

We next discuss the case
m
even.
Since

(\bar{z}_{2m,2}\otimes 2)\circ\mathfrak{s}(x)=\delta^{\prime}(x)\in\langle\Theta_{2m}^{\geq 1}\rangle

for
x\in\langle\Theta_{2m-1}^{\geq 1,s}\rangle
, the above map
f
factors through
\pi\circ\mathfrak{s}_{2m-1,1}
as follows:

\textstyle{\mathcal{A}_{2m-1,1}^{c}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{2m-1,1}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y_{2m-1}\mathcal{I}\mathcal{C}/Y_{2m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{z}_{2m,2}\otimes 2}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{A}_{2m,2}^{c}/\langle\Theta_{2m}^{\geq 1}\rangle)\otimes\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{bd}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}_{2m-2,1}^{c}\otimes\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z},}
\textstyle{(Y_{2m-1}\mathcal{I}\mathcal{C}/Y_{2m})/\mathfrak{s}(\langle\Theta_{2m-1}^{\geq 1,s}\rangle)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

where the module
\langle\Theta_{2m-1}^{\geq 1,s}\rangle
is defined in Section
1
.
Hence we have
\operatorname{Ker}(\pi\circ\mathfrak{s}_{2m-1,1})\subset\operatorname{Ker}f
.
Now, it suffices to show that

\left\langle\mathfrak{mh}(x)+\mathfrak{mh}(\iota(x))\Bigm|x\in\overrightarrow{N}_{2m}^{e\geq 1}\right\rangle\cap\operatorname{Ker}f=\{0\},

where
\overrightarrow{N}_{2m}^{e\geq 1}=\{x\in\overrightarrow{N}^{\prime\prime}_{2m}\mid e(x)\geq 1,\ \iota(x)\in\overrightarrow{N}^{\prime\prime}_{2m}\}
.
Note that any
x\in\overrightarrow{N}_{2m}^{e\geq 1}
can be uniquely written as

x=O(\underbrace{w,\bar{w},\dots,w,\bar{w}}_{2^{e}}\uparrow\underbrace{w,\bar{w},\dots,w,\bar{w}}_{2^{e}})

for some word
w\in\{1^{\pm},\dots,g^{\pm}\}^{m/2^{e}}
with
w\neq\bar{w}
, where
\bar{w}
denotes the word obtained from
w
by reversing the order (see Example
5.5
below).
By definition, we have

f(\mathfrak{mh}(x)+\mathfrak{mh}(\iota(x)))=\mathfrak{mht}(x)+\mathfrak{mht}(\iota(x))\in\langle B_{2m-2}^{s}\rangle\otimes\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}

for
x\in\overrightarrow{N}_{2m}^{e\geq 1}
.
This element is contained in

\langle B_{2m-2}^{s,\mathrm{period}}\rangle\otimes\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\cong\langle B_{m-1}^{s}\rangle\cong H^{\otimes m/2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}.

See
[
15
, Lemma 5.3]
for the definitions of the superscript “period” and the first isomorphism, and here recall Remark
3.12
.
Under these isomorphisms, the image of
x=O(w,\bar{w},\dots,w,\bar{w}\uparrow w,\bar{w},\dots,w,\bar{w})
is
w\bar{w}\cdots w\bar{w}+\bar{w}w\cdots\bar{w}w
, where each term consists of
2^{e-1}
words.
Since these two terms are linearly independent in
H^{\otimes m/2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
, this completes the proof.
∎

Example 5.5
.

For
a\neq b
, let
x=O(a,b,b,a,a,b,b,a\uparrow a,b,b,a,a,b,b,a)\in\overrightarrow{N}_{16}
.
Then
w=ab
,
\bar{w}=ba
, and
x
is sent to
abba+baab\in H^{\otimes 4}\otimes\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
.

6.
Kernel of
\mathfrak{s}
restricted to
\mathcal{A}_{n,l}^{c}
for
l>1

We introduce a weight system, and prove Theorem
6.9
which gives lower bounds on the ranks of the
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-modules
\operatorname{Ker}(\mathfrak{s}|_{\operatorname{tor}\mathcal{A}_{2k+1,k}^{c}})
and
\operatorname{Im}(\mathfrak{s}|_{\operatorname{tor}\mathcal{A}_{2k+1,k}^{c}})
for
k\geq 0
.

One usually constructs a weight system from a Lie algebra over
\mathbb{C}
(see
[
2
, Section 6.3]
for instance).
However, we need to define it over
\mathbb{Z}
.
Let
R
be a commutative unital ring, and let
\mathfrak{g}
be a free
R
-module with a basis
\{e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}
.
Suppose
c_{ijk}\in R
(
i,j,k=1,\dots,d
) satisfy that

\displaystyle c_{ijk}=-c_{jik},

(6.1)

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{d}(c_{ijm}c_{mkl}-c_{lim}c_{mjk}+c_{ljm}c_{mik})=0,

(6.2)

\displaystyle c_{iik}=0,

(6.3)

\displaystyle c_{ijk}=c_{jki}=c_{kij}.

(6.4)

These conditions arise from a Lie algebra as follows.
Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a Lie algebra over
R
with basis
\{e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}
.
(While
R
is not a field but a ring, we use the terminology “Lie algebra”.)
Define the structure constants
c_{ijk}\in R
by
[e_{i},e_{j}]=\sum_{k}c_{ijk}e_{k}
and suppose they satisfy
\delta([e_{i},e_{k}],e_{j})=\delta(e_{i},[e_{k},e_{j}])
(see
[
2
, Appendix A.1]
for example).
Here
\delta
is the bilinear form defined by
\delta(e_{i},e_{j})=\delta_{ij}
, where
\delta_{ij}
denotes the Kronecker delta.
Then one can check that the
c_{ijk}
’s satisfy the conditions (
6.1
)–(
6.4
).

Example 6.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a semi-simple Lie algebra over
\mathbb{C}
.
Then the structure constants
c_{ijk}
associated with an orthonormal basis with respect to the Killing form satisfy the conditions (
6.1
)–(
6.4
).

Let
H_{R}=H_{1}(\Sigma_{g,1};R)
and identify
H_{R}
with the
R
-module
R\{1^{\pm},\dots,g^{\pm}\}
.
For an
R
-module
M
, let
S(M)
denote the symmetric algebra over
R
.
Recall that
d=\operatorname{rank}_{R}\mathfrak{g}
.

Definition 6.2
.

The
weight system
associated with
\mathfrak{g}
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-module homomorphism
W_{\mathfrak{g}}\colon\mathcal{A}_{n}\to S(H_{R}\otimes_{R}\mathfrak{g})
defined as follows.
Let
J\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
be a Jacobi diagram, and let
v_{1},\dots,v_{m}
(resp.
w_{1},\dots,w_{n}
) be the univalent (resp. trivalent) vertices of
J
.
Put labels
\{1,\dots,d\}
on the edges of
J
, and let
c(w_{l})=c_{ijk}
if the edges adjacent to
w_{l}
are labeled by
i,j,k
in the cyclic order of
w_{l}
.
Then, define
W_{\mathfrak{g}}(J)\in S^{m}(H_{R}\otimes_{R}\mathfrak{g})
by

W_{\mathfrak{g}}(J)=\sum c(w_{1})\cdots c(w_{n})(l(v_{1})\otimes e_{j_{1}})\cdots(l(v_{m})\otimes e_{j_{m}}),

where
j_{i}\in\{1,\dots,d\}
is the label of the edge adjacent to
v_{i}
, and the sum is taken over all the ways of assigning labels to edges.
Note that
c(w_{l})
depends only on the cyclic order by (
6.4
).

In other words, for a Jacobi diagram
J
without strut, we first put
t=\sum_{i,j,k}c_{ijk}e_{i}\otimes e_{j}\otimes e_{k}\in\mathfrak{g}^{\otimes 3}
at each trivalent vertex.
Note that
t
is invariant under cyclic permutation by (
6.4
).
Next, using the bilinear form
\delta
, we take the contraction of the form

\mathfrak{g}^{\otimes 3}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{\otimes 3}\to\mathfrak{g}^{\otimes 4},\ (e_{i}\otimes e_{j}\otimes e_{k},e_{i^{\prime}}\otimes e_{j^{\prime}}\otimes e_{k^{\prime}})\mapsto\delta(e_{i},e_{i^{\prime}})e_{j}\otimes e_{k}\otimes e_{j^{\prime}}\otimes e_{k^{\prime}}

along every edge connecting two trivalent vertices (see
[
2
, Section 6.2.1]
for instance).
Then, consider the tensor product
l(v_{i})\otimes e_{j_{i}}
at each univalent vertex
v_{i}
, and take the summation of the products of
\delta_{ij}
’s and
l(v_{i})\otimes e_{j_{i}}
.

The weight system
W_{\mathfrak{g}}
is well-defined since one can check that
W_{\mathfrak{g}}
preserves the AS, IHX, and self-loop relations by (
6.1
), (
6.2
) and (
6.3
), respectively.
For example, to prove that
W_{\mathfrak{g}}
preserves (
6.2
), we consider

where the central edge in each diagram is labeled by
m
which runs over
1,\dots,d
.

Example 6.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}=\left\langle e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{Z}}
and

c_{ijk}=\begin{cases}\operatorname{sgn}(ijk)&\text{if $\#\{i,j,k\}=3$,}\\
0&\text{if $\#\{i,j,k\}<3$.}\end{cases}

One can directly check that these
c_{ijk}
’s satisfy (
6.1
)–(
6.4
).
Also, it arises from
\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{C})
with basis
\{(E+F)/2\sqrt{-1},H/2\sqrt{-1},(E-F)/2\}
, where

E=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},\ H=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix},\ F=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}.

Here we give an example of computation.
For
J=O(a,b)\in\mathcal{A}_{2,1}^{c}
, we have

W_{\mathfrak{g}}(O(a,b))=-2(a\otimes e_{1})(b\otimes e_{1})-2(a\otimes e_{2})(b\otimes e_{2})-2(a\otimes e_{3})(b\otimes e_{3}).

Note that the computation of this weight system corresponds to counting edge-colorings (with sign) by three colors
\{1,2,3\}
such that all the three colors appear around each trivalent vertex.

Example 6.4
.

The weight system in Example
6.3
enables us show that the set
\{T(a,b,c,c,b,a),T(b,c,a,a,c,b)\}
extends to a basis of
\mathcal{A}_{4,0}^{c}
when
a,b,c\in\{1^{\pm},\dots,g^{\pm}\}
are mutually distinct.
Indeed, one can check that the coefficients of
(a\otimes e_{1})^{2}(b\otimes e_{2})^{2}(c\otimes e_{2})^{2}
and
(a\otimes e_{1})^{2}(b\otimes e_{1})^{2}(c\otimes e_{2})^{2}
in
W_{\mathfrak{g}}(T(a,b,c,c,b,a))\in S^{6}(H\otimes\mathfrak{g})
are 1 and 0, respectively.
On the other hand, the coefficients in
W_{\mathfrak{g}}(T(b,c,a,a,c,b))
are 0 and 1, respectively.

In the rest of this section, we consider the weight system in Example
6.3
, and simply write
W
.
By Lemma
6.5
below, it induces a homomorphism

W_{m}\colon\mathcal{A}_{n}\otimes\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}\to S(H\otimes\mathbb{Z}e_{m})\otimes\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z},\ J\mapsto\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{pr}_{m}\circ W(J),

where
\mathrm{pr}_{m}\colon S(H\otimes\mathfrak{g})\to S(H\otimes\mathbb{Z}e_{m})
denotes the projection.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
m\in\{1,2,3\}
and
n\geq 1
.
The image of the composite map
\mathcal{A}_{n}\xrightarrow{W}S(H\otimes\mathfrak{g})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{pr}_{m}}S(H\otimes\mathbb{Z}e_{m})
is included in
2S(H\otimes\mathbb{Z}e_{m})
.

Proof.

We may assume
m=1
, and use the comment at the end of Example
6.3
.
For each edge-coloring of a Jacobi diagram
J\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
such that any edge adjacent to a univalent vertex is colored by
1
, there is an edge-coloring obtained by replacing all 2’s (resp. 3’s) with 3 (resp. 2).
Therefore, the coefficient of
x\otimes e_{1}
is even for
x\in H
.
∎

We now give a useful relation between the weight system and the map
\mathfrak{bu}
, which is essentially written, for example, in
[
2
, Lemma 6.15 and Remark 6.16]
.

Lemma 6.6
.

W(\mathfrak{bu}^{k}(J))=(-1)^{k}W(J)
for any
k\geq 0
, where
\mathfrak{bu}^{k}\colon\mathcal{A}_{n,l}^{c}\to\mathcal{A}_{n+2k,l+k}^{c}
denotes the
k
times composition of
\mathfrak{bu}
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the case
k=1
.
By the comment at the end of Example
6.3
, for each edge-coloring of
J
, there is a unique edge-coloring of
\mathfrak{bu}(J)
extending it.
This gives a bijection between the edge-colorings of
J
and those of
\mathfrak{bu}(J)
, with opposite sign.
This implies
W(\mathfrak{bu}(J))=-W(J)
.
∎

Corollary
6.7
and Proposition
6.8
below are keys to prove Theorem
6.9
.

Corollary 6.7
.

For any
a,b\in\{1^{\pm},\dots,g^{\pm}\}
,

W_{1}\circ\bar{z}_{2k+2,k+1}\circ\mathfrak{s}\circ\mathfrak{bu}^{k}(T(a,b,a))=\frac{1}{2}(a\otimes e_{1})(b\otimes e_{1})\in S(H\otimes\mathbb{Z}e_{1})\otimes\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}.

In particular,
\bar{z}_{2k+2,k+1}
is non-trivial.

Proof.

Let
J=T(a,b,a)
.
By
[
15
, Theorem 1]
, we have

\bar{z}_{2k+2,k+1}\circ\mathfrak{s}\circ\mathfrak{bu}^{k}(J)=\frac{1}{2}\delta^{\prime\prime}(\mathfrak{bu}^{k}(J))=\mathfrak{bu}^{k}\left(\frac{1}{2}\delta^{\prime\prime}(J)\right).

If follows from Lemma
6.6
and
\delta^{\prime\prime}(J)=O(a,b)
that
W_{1}\circ\mathfrak{bu}^{k}(\frac{1}{2}\delta^{\prime\prime}(J))=W_{1}(\frac{1}{2}O(a,b))
.
Finally, this is equal to
\frac{1}{2}(a\otimes e_{1})(b\otimes e_{1})
by Example
6.3
.
∎

Proposition 6.8
.

\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{bu}^{k}(T(a,b,a)))=\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{bu}^{k}(T(b,a,b)))\in Y_{2k+1}\mathcal{I}\mathcal{C}/Y_{2k+2}
.

Proof.

Let
J\in\mathbb{Z}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{2k,k}^{c}
be a lift of
\mathfrak{bu}^{k-1}(O(a,b))
.
By Corollary
3.7
, we have

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}(J)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}(\mathrm{rev}(J))

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}(J)+\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{bu}^{k-1}(O(a,b,a)))+\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{bu}^{k-1}(O(b,a,b))).

This completes the proof since
\mathfrak{bu}^{k-1}(O(a,b,a))=\mathfrak{bu}^{k}(T(a,b,a))
.
∎

In Appendix
A
, we give an alternative proof of Proposition
6.8
without using Corollary
3.7
.

Theorem 6.9
.

For
g\geq 1
and
k\geq 0
, the ranks of the
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-modules
\operatorname{Ker}(\mathfrak{s}|_{\operatorname{tor}\mathcal{A}_{2k+1,k}^{c}})
and
\operatorname{Im}(\mathfrak{s}|_{\operatorname{tor}\mathcal{A}_{2k+1,k}^{c}})
are respectively greater than or equal to
g(2g-1)
and
g(2g+1)
.
Furthermore, the ranks are exactly
g(2g-1)
and
g(2g+1)
when
k=0,1,2
.

Proof.

In this proof, we write
\mathfrak{s}_{2k+1,k}
for the restriction map
\mathfrak{s}|_{\operatorname{tor}\mathcal{A}_{2k+1,k}^{c}}
.
It follows from Proposition
6.8
that

\mathfrak{bu}^{k}(T(a,b,a))-\mathfrak{bu}^{k}(T(b,a,b))\in\operatorname{Ker}\mathfrak{s}.

Here, by Corollary
6.7
, one has
\mathfrak{bu}^{k}(T(a,b,a))\neq 0
.
Comparing labels, we conclude that the above element is non-trivial and
\operatorname{rank}\operatorname{Ker}\mathfrak{s}_{2k+1,k}\geq\binom{2g}{2}=g(2g-1)
.
Also, Corollary
6.7
implies that
\operatorname{rank}\operatorname{Im}\mathfrak{s}_{2k+1,k}\geq g(2g-1)+2g=g(2g+1)
.

We finally show that the above inequality is an equality when
k=0,1,2
.
Since we have the exact sequence

0\to\operatorname{Ker}\mathfrak{s}_{2k+1,k}\to\operatorname{tor}\mathcal{A}_{2k+1,k}^{c}\to\operatorname{Im}\mathfrak{s}_{2k+1,k}\to 0,

it suffices to see that
\operatorname{rank}\operatorname{tor}\mathcal{A}_{2k+1,k}^{c}\leq 4g^{2}
.
By Lemma
4.4
, the cases
k=1,2
are reduced to the case
k=0
.
Then the proof is completed by the fact that
\operatorname{tor}\mathcal{A}_{1,0}^{c}=\left\langle T(a,b,a)\mid a,b\in\{1^{\pm},\dots,g^{\pm}\}\right\rangle
.
∎

Appendix A
Alternative proof of Proposition
6.8

In this appendix, we give an alternative proof of Proposition
6.8
by generalizing the proof of
[
15
, Lemma 6.6(1)]
.
The key ingredients are twisted leaves and the zip construction. Every box in a clasper
G
has one output end and two input ends.
To apply the zip construction to
G
, we need to specify a marking on
G
, which is a set of input ends of boxes.
In the following proof, we do not specify markings but simply indicate which boxes are used.
Here we review a useful lemma.

We first apply Lemma
A.1
to the leftmost twisted leaf, and then a new box is created.
Applying

, Move 11]
to this box
k
times from left to right, we obtain the equivalent clasper with
2k+1
boxes

Apply the zip construction to the
k+1
boxes with odd numbers so that one obtains a graph clasper
G
of degree
2k+1
arising from the left-top input edge of Box
2k+1
after using
[
8
, Move 2]
.
Hence, by
[
11
, Lemma A.1]
(or
[
8
, Moves 11 and 12]
),
G
can be separated from the leaf connected to the right-top input edge of Box
2k-1
up to
Y_{2k+2}
-equivalence.
We next apply the same zip construction backward, and then we delete the leaf and Box
2k-1
by
[
8
, Move 3]
:

Here we choose one of the
2k
boxes except the rightmost one, and apply
[
8
, Move 11]
to the box
2k+1
times so that one obtains a graph clasper of degree
2k+1
with a twisted leaf, which is deleted by
[
11
, Lemma A.5]
up to
Y_{2k+2}
-equivalence.
Eventually, we obtain the clasper with a box

Here we use
[
8
, Move 5 or 6]
, and get the right-hand side
\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{bu}^{k}(T(b,a,b)))
of the statement.

On the other hand, if we start the above procedure from the rightmost twisted leaf, then we get the left-hand side
\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{bu}^{k}(T(a,b,a)))
.
∎
